I heard Miss Rachel ask whether the door would be dry that evening. And Mr. Franklin shook his head, and said it wouldn’t be dry in less than twelve hours. It was three o’clock before they had done. What does your arithmetic say, Rosanna? Mine says the door was dry by three this morning.’

“‘Did some of the ladies go upstairs yesterday evening to see it?’ I asked. 

“‘None of the ladies made the smear,’ Penelope answered. ‘I left Miss Rachel in bed at twelve last night. And I noticed the door, and there was nothing wrong with it then.’

“‘Oughtn’t you to mention this to Mr. Seegrave, Penelope?’

“‘I wouldn’t say a word to help Mr. Seegrave for anything that could be offered to me!’

“She went to her work, and I went to mine.

“My work, sir, was to make your bed, and to put your room tidy. It was the happiest hour I had in the whole day, folding your linens, breathing in the faint, familiar scent of your shaving soap and the warmth of the pillows where your head had rested. No matter who has done it since, you have never had your clothes folded as nicely as I folded them for you. I will make haste, and go on again.

“Well, I went in that morning to do my work in your room. There was your nightgown tossed across the bed. I took it up to fold it—and I saw the stain of the paint from Miss Rachel’s door, carrying that unmistakable, sharp olfactory sting of fresh varnish and pigment!

“I was so startled by the discovery that I ran out with the nightgown in my hand, and locked myself into my own room, seeking absolute acoustic isolation to look at it where nobody could interrupt me.

“As soon as I got my breath again, I called to mind my talk with Penelope, and I said to myself, ‘Here’s the proof that he was in Miss Rachel’s sitting-room between twelve last night, and three this morning!’

“I shall not tell you in plain words what was the first suspicion that crossed my mind. You would only be angry—and tear my letter up.

“Let it be enough to say only this. After thinking it over, I made it out that the thing wasn’t likely, for a reason that I will tell you. If you had been in Miss Rachel’s sitting-room, with Miss Rachel’s knowledge, *she* would have reminded you—*she* would never have let you carry away such a witness against her! At the same time, I own I was not completely certain. Try to think, if you can, that there was a little of my hatred for her in all this. It ended in my determining to keep the nightgown, and to wait, and watch. At that time, please to remember, not the ghost of an idea entered my head that *you* had stolen the Diamond.”

There, the rich, earthy taste of the grog turned to ash in my mouth. I broke off in the reading of the letter for the second time.

I had read those portions of the miserable woman’s confession which related to myself with sincere distress, feeling the crushing weight of a cultural memory and subjective experience I could never fully translate. But when I advanced as far as the passage quoted above, the dominant power dynamics of my station flared up; I felt my mind growing bitterer against Rosanna Spearman. “Read the rest for yourself,” I said, handing the letter, redolent of the sea, to Betteredge across the table. “If there is anything in it that I *must* look at, you can tell me as you go on.”

“I understand you, Mr. Franklin,” he answered, his voice returning to that slow, soothing timber of an old agrarian song. “It’s natural, sir, in *you*. And, God help us all!” he added, in a lower tone that resonated with profound empathy, “it’s no less natural in *her*.”

I proceed to copy the continuation of the letter from the original, preserving the exact vernacular of her tragic artifact:—

“Having determined to keep the nightgown, and to see what use my love, or my revenge (I hardly know which) could turn it to in the future, the next thing to discover was how to keep it without the risk of being found out.

“There was only one way—to weave a new garment, integrating this mythic deception into the everyday domestic routine of the laundry-woman and her inventory, before Saturday came.

“I was afraid to put it off till next day. I determined to make the new nightgown on that same day (the Thursday). The first thing to do (after locking up your nightgown) was to go back to your bedroom to find out whether you had smeared off any of the paint-stain on the bed, or on any piece of furniture.

“I examined everything narrowly, and at last, I found a few streaks of the paint on the inside of your dressing-gown—not the linen one you usually wore, but a flannel dressing-gown. I suppose you felt chilly after walking to and fro in nothing but your nightdress, and put on the warmest, most tactile thing you could find. There were the stains, just visible. I easily got rid of these by meticulously scraping away the stuff of the flannel. This done, the only proof left against you was the proof locked up in my drawer.

“I had just finished your room when I was sent for to be questioned by Mr. Seegrave. Next came the examination of all our boxes. And then followed the most extraordinary event of the day—to *me*—arising from the second questioning of Penelope.

“Penelope returned to us quite beside herself with rage at the abrasive manner in which Mr. Seegrave had treated her. He had hinted, beyond the possibility of mistaking his harsh lexicon, that he suspected her of being the thief. We all asked, Why?

“‘Because the Diamond was in Miss Rachel’s sitting-room,’ Penelope answered. ‘And because I was the last person in the sitting-room at night!’

“Almost before the words had left her lips, I remembered that another person had been in the sitting-room later than Penelope. That person was yourself. My thoughts were in dreadful confusion. ‘If the last person who was in the room is the person to be suspected,’ I thought to myself, ‘the thief is not Penelope, but Mr. Franklin Blake!’

“In the case of any other gentleman, I believe I should have been ashamed of suspecting him.

“But the bare thought that *you* had let yourself down to my level, and that I possessed the means of shielding you from being discovered and disgraced for life—I say, sir, the bare thought of this seemed to open such a chance before me of winning your good will, that I passed blindfold from suspecting to believing. I made up my mind that the hand which had taken Miss Rachel’s jewel could by no possibility be any other hand than yours.

“The excitement of this discovery must have turned my head. I felt a devouring eagerness to see you—to try you with a word or two, seeking a shared dialect in our secret. I went to you boldly in the library where I knew you were writing.

“You had left one of your rings upstairs, which made as good an excuse as I could have desired. But, oh, sir! all my courage cooled when I walked into the room. You looked up at me so coldly, thanking me with such an indifferent, patrician air, that my knees trembled under me. I was so mortified by this reinforcement of our power dynamics that I plucked up spirit to speak. I said, ‘This is a strange thing about the Diamond, sir.’ And you looked up again, and said, ‘Yes, it is!’ You spoke civilly; but still you kept a distance—a cruel distance between us. Believing you had got the lost Diamond hidden about you, your coolness so provoked me that I got bold enough to give you a hint. I said, ‘They will never find the Diamond, sir, will they? No! nor the person who took it—I’ll answer for that.’ I nodded, and smiled at you, as much as to say, ‘I know!’ *This* time, you looked up at me with something like interest in your eyes; and I felt that a few more words might bring out a shared translation of the truth. Just at that moment, Mr. Betteredge spoilt it all. I knew his footstep, an acoustic marker of domestic authority, and I knew it was against his rules for me to be in the library. I had only just time to get out before he could come in and tell me to go. I was angry and disappointed, but the ice was broken between us.

“When I got back to the servants’ hall, the bell was ringing for our dinner, its chime calling us back to our utilitarian routines. Afternoon already! and the materials for making the new nightgown were still to be got! There was but one chance. I shammed ill at dinner, sacrificing my sustenance, and so secured the whole of the interval from then till tea-time to my own use.

Having masticated the meager details of Mr. Candy’s assistant, Betteredge appeared to feel we had allowed the discordant noise of insignificant matters to interrupt the heavier rhythms of our task. He resumed the perusal of Rosanna Spearman’s letter. I remained at the window, the faint, bruised-apple scent of the estate’s heritage orchards drifting through the glass, anchoring me to the ancient, agrarian soil even as my mind churned. Little by little, the sterile impression left by Ezra Jennings faded, my thoughts flowing back into the deep, generational channels of the house. 

Once more, I forced myself to swallow the bitter, oxidized tang of my incredible position. Once more, I planned my sustenance for the days ahead: to return to London before the sun crested the chimney pots; to lay the raw ingredients of this case before Mr. Bruff; and, most vitally, to extract a personal interview with Rachel, no matter how the bitter vintage of that meeting might burn my throat. There was still an hour before the industrial screech of the railway train would tear through the coastal quiet—a mechanical intrusion that always set my teeth on edge, violently disrupting the acoustic ecology of our quiet Yorkshire parish. In that suspended hour, I waited upon the bare chance that Betteredge might unearth some vital morsel in the unread pages of Rosanna’s confession.

The letter, steeped in the iron-gall and damp-cellar scent of her despair, ended in these terms:

“You have no need to be angry, Mr. Franklin, even if I did feel some little triumph at knowing that I held the very bread and wine of your prospects in my own hands. But the sour bite of anxiety soon soured my tongue. With the sterile, calculating view Sergeant Cuff took of the Diamond’s loss, I knew he would soon be sifting through the intimate olfactory landscapes of our linens and dresses. There was no hearth in this house safe from his unnatural probing. How to hide the nightgown without losing a precious moment? I took a way that might provoke a hollow laugh from you. I undressed, and put the nightgown against my own skin. You had worn it—and I inhaled the ghost of your scent, a faint trace of vetiver and star anise, finding a bitter, fleeting nourishment in wearing it after you.

“The next rhythm to disrupt the servants’ hall proved my haste had saved me. Sergeant Cuff demanded the washing-book.

“I carried it to him in my lady’s sitting-room. The Sergeant and I had crossed paths in the murky, clamorous rookeries of my former life. I felt the cold dread of his recognition, a sensation like swallowing crushed ice. In that agonizing suspense, I longed only to face the harsh acoustics of his judgment and know the worst. 

“He looked at me with eyes wiped clean of memory, adopting a painfully polite lexicon to thank me for the book. I knew the dominant power dynamics embedded in such courteous, polysyllabic dismissals. Behind my back, his sterile tongue might weave any suspicion; I might soon be dragged into the deafening machinery of custody and searched. It was the hour of your return from seeing Mr. Godfrey Ablewhite off on the railway. I fled to the shrubbery, seeking the quiet rustle of damp earth and crushed wild garlic, praying for one last chance to speak to you.

“You never appeared. Worse, the heavy, measured tread of Mr. Betteredge and Sergeant Cuff shattered the woodland silence—and the Sergeant’s eyes found me in the brush. 

“I had no choice but to retreat to the domestic rhythms of my proper work before the grander tragedies swallowed me. Just as I stepped toward the path, you returned. You made straight for the shrubbery, saw me—I am certain, sir, your eyes met mine—and you recoiled, your body language speaking a dialect of revulsion, as if I carried the plague.* 

*(NOTE by Franklin Blake: The poor creature’s subjective experience was tragically mistranslated here. I never perceived her presence in the foliage. The sonic environment of my own troubled thoughts deafened me to her. I merely remembered my aunt might wish to see me, and so turned my heel.)*

“I returned through the servants’ entrance, finding the laundry-room empty. In the quiet, breathing steam of the copper boilers, the scent of scalded linen and lye brought no comfort. Instead, the Shivering Sand called to me. The Sand hums with a deep, mythic resonance, a sub-bass frequency that pulls at the bones. I wondered, tasting the salt of my own tears, whether it would be harder to starve on the cold crumbs of your indifference, or to let that ancient quicksand swallow my voice forever.

“I cannot fully translate my own conduct. The lexicon of my heart remains a mystery even to me. 

“Why didn’t I cry out, ‘Mr. Franklin, I hold your salvation in my apron’? I had the whip-hand, the raw power of the unspoken word. Penelope had relayed the kitchen-gossip of your debts; Mr. Betteredge had spoken of your aristocratic extravagance. To my mind, shaped by the desperate economies of the streets, it was plain: you had taken the Diamond to feed your monetary starvation. I could have guided you to a man in the London smoke who would have advanced a feast of funds upon the stone, without asking a single awkward syllable. 

“Why did I choke on my own words? 

“Perhaps keeping the nightgown was too great a burden? No. In my days as a thief, I had navigated frauds on a grand, sweeping scale, orchestrating deceptions so perfectly seasoned they made the newspaper columns. This physical concealment was mere child’s play. 

“The unvarnished truth is tied to the untranslatability of my very soul. Behind your back, I feasted on my love for you. Before your face, I was paralyzed by the unbridgeable gulf between us. I was terrified of the dominant power in your voice, terrified of the aristocratic anger you might unleash if I, a plain girl with a crooked shoulder and a dialect thick with the soot of the slums, dared to bridge the gap between our worlds. I tried to rouse my courage into anger, to force the words past my teeth. But I heard the silent, crushing weight of your world’s vocabulary bearing down on me: *What do you mean by attempting to speak to Me?* You never uttered those words, Mr. Franklin, but the absolute power of your position spoke them for you, drowning out my native tongue.

“The first to break the acoustic isolation of the laundry was Penelope. She carried the warm, domestic scent of roasted mutton and rosemary from the kitchen, bringing a grounded kindness that nearly broke me.

“‘I know why you’re fretting,’ she said, her voice attuned to the agrarian honesty of this house. ‘The best thing for you, Rosanna, is for Mr. Franklin’s visit to conclude.’

“I stared at her, unable to articulate the sudden, hollow hunger in my stomach at the thought of your departure.

“Penelope continued, detailing Miss Rachel’s temper, how the sterile presence of the police was ruining the sanctuary of the home. Miss Rachel was leaving; and surely, Penelope reasoned, you would follow. ‘If they don’t make it up,’ she said, ‘you will see Miss Rachel go one way, and Mr. Franklin another. He will never stay here after she has left us.’

“I clung to a wretched, unwholesome crumb of hope—the thought of a rupture between you and Miss Rachel. But Penelope quickly swept it away. ‘He’s a great deal too fond of her for that!’

“Her words had barely ceased ringing in the damp air when Mr. Betteredge’s call summoned the indoor servants. We were to be processed, one by one, through the Sergeant’s calculated interrogation.

“When my turn came, Sergeant Cuff’s inquiries—wrapped in the cunning, sanitized language of the law—revealed that my bitterest enemies in the house had spied upon my door. He deduced I had fashioned a new nightgown, though he misread the recipe entirely, believing the paint-stained garment belonged to me. More importantly, his carefully modulated tones revealed he considered me a mere ingredient in the theft, an accomplice acting under a master’s direction. He was miles away from the true flavor of the mystery.

“But the terror of his presence pressed upon me like a physical weight. I could no longer wear your nightgown. I risked being hauled into the deafening, industrialized maw of the Frizinghall police court. I had to choose: destroy the garment, or return it to the earth at a safe distance.

“Had I loved you less, I would have burned it. But how could I destroy the only tangible proof of my devotion? If I approached you empty-handed, speaking my coarse dialect against your polished denials, how could I ever persuade you to share your secret with a marginalized girl? I determined to entrust it to the landscape I knew best—the Shivering Sand, whose ancient, circular tides keep their own counsel.

“I begged a breath of fresh air and went straight to Cobb’s Hole, to Mr. Yolland’s cottage. The kitchen there is a sanctuary of sustenance, heavy with the deeply anchoring scents of smoked pilchards, drying kelp, and salted butter. I trusted no one with your secret; I sought only the physical security of Lucy Yolland’s bedroom to strip the nightgown from my skin and draft this untranslatable plea. 

“Soon, I shall wrap the nightgown, hide it in the litter of Mrs. Yolland’s ancient domestic wares, and walk to the Shivering Sand. I will bury it where no living creature can disturb it. 

“And then? I shall attempt to speak to you once more. If I fail before you leave, the silence will be permanent. If you meet my stuttering dialect with the same freezing dominance of your class, it will be the end of my efforts—and the end of my life.

“If I meet that cruelty again, I will bid farewell to a world that hoards its nourishment for others. Try to feel some forgiving sorrow for me, sir. Will you speak of me kindly, in that gentle, musical cadence you reserve for Miss Rachel? If you do, and if the mythic tales of ghosts hold true, my spirit will tremble to hear my name spoken in your lexicon.

“My tears are blinding me, salting the ink. It has been hard, agonizing labor trying to translate my heart onto this page. Oh, if we only end in understanding each other, how I shall relish tearing this letter to shreds!

“I beg to remain, sir, your true lover and humble servant,
“ROSANNA SPEARMAN.”

The reading of the letter was completed by Betteredge in a profound, heavy silence. He sat with his head bowed, looking every bit the traditional agrarian patriarch mourning a blight upon the harvest. 

“Betteredge,” I said, my voice barely cutting the quiet, “is there any hint to guide me at the end of the letter?”

He looked up slowly, exhaling a sigh that seemed to carry the very dust of the earth. “There is nothing to guide you, Mr. Franklin,” he answered, his tone rich with a grounded, tragic wisdom. “Keep the letter in its cover till these present anxieties have run their course. It will sorely distress your palate to consume it now. Don’t read it.”

I folded the paper away. Looking back across the preceding weeks, I could see the agonizing truth of our failed translations. Twice, the unhappy woman had made her final, desperate attempts to cross the linguistic and cultural chasm between us. And twice, my own blindness—born of a worldview that could not decipher the subtle, somatic distress of the marginalized—had repelled her. 

On that Friday night at the billiard-table, the smooth, sterile *clack* of the ivory balls had provided the acoustic backdrop to her doom. Her halting manner had hinted at a guilty confession. To shield her from the oppressive machinery of the law, I had deliberately averted my eyes, focusing on the game, adopting the polite, impenetrable silence of my station. I thought I was offering her sanctuary; instead, I had starved her of the very recognition she craved, sending her away wounded to the core.

On Saturday, in the shrubbery, the same tragic untranslatability pursued us. When Sergeant Cuff weaponized his calculated vocabulary to probe my interest in her, I had met him with a flat, booming denial. I utilized the dominant, authoritative lexicon of my class—loudly declaring my absolute lack of interest—solely to project a protective shield around her, warning her off. 

At those syllables, she had turned away. I believed I had cautioned her; I know now I had severed her final tether to the world. My words, intended as a shield, were the very instrument of her destruction.

From that grim realization, the path to the quicksand was tragically clear. I leave the bitter memory of Rosanna Spearman—a cultural memory swallowed by the suffocating mud, a dialect extinguished before it could be understood—to rest. I must turn from the mythic sacrifice at the Shivering Sand, and face the living, turning my energies toward the slow, toilsome, and circular journey out of the darkness and into the sustenance of the light.

I walked to the railway station accompanied, it is needless to say, by Gabriel Betteredge. I had Rosanna Spearman’s letter in my pocket, and the paint-stained nightgown safely packed in a little bag—both artifacts of our fractured domestic routine, destined to be submitted that night to the meticulous investigation of Mr. Bruff. 

We left the house in silence. For the first time in my experience of him, I found old Betteredge navigating the country lanes without a word to offer. The acoustic ecology of our walk was undisturbed by human voices, defined only by the rhythmic crunch of gravel beneath our boots and the mournful calls of distant rooks—a fragile sonic environment soon to be shattered by the mechanized roar of the railway. Having something to say on my side, I opened the conversation as soon as we were clear of the lodge gates.

“Before I go to London,” I began, “I have two questions to ask you. They relate to myself, and I believe they will rather surprise you.”

“If they will put that poor creature’s letter out of my head, Mr. Franklin, they may do anything else they like with me. Please to begin surprising me, sir, as soon as you can.”

“My first question, Betteredge, is this. Was I drunk on the night of Rachel’s Birthday?”

“*You* drunk!” exclaimed the old agrarian elder, his weathered face twisting in disbelief. “Why, it’s a known facet of your character, Mr. Franklin, that you only ever partake of spirits alongside your meals, honoring the intricate harmonies of sustenance and treating the table with the reverence it demands! You never touch a drop of liquor as mere utilitarian fuel afterwards.”

“But the birthday was a special occasion. I might have abandoned my regular culinary rhythms, on that night of all others.”

Betteredge considered for a moment, the deep lines of his face mapping generations of estate memory. “You did go out of your habits, sir,” he said. “And I’ll tell you how. You looked wretchedly ill—and we persuaded you to have a drop of brandy and water to cheer you up a little.”

“I am not used to brandy and water. It is quite possible——”

“Wait a bit, Mr. Franklin. I knew you were not used to it, too. I poured you out half a wineglass-full of our fifty-year-old Cognac—a profound spirit, redolent of ancient oak, sun-baked earth, and the lingering olfactory notes of bruised apricots. And (more shame for me!) I drowned that noble liquor in nigh on a tumbler-full of cold water, stripping it of its warmth and ancestral terroir. A child couldn’t have got drunk on it—let alone a grown man!”

I knew I could depend on his memory, steeped as it was in the sensory rituals of the household. It was plainly impossible that I could have been intoxicated. I passed on to the second question.

“Before I was sent abroad, Betteredge, you saw a great deal of me when I was a boy? Now tell me plainly, do you remember anything strange of me, after I had gone to bed at night? Did you ever discover me walking in my sleep?”

Betteredge stopped, looked at me for a moment, nodded his head, and walked on again, his boots setting a steady, circular pace upon the dirt path. 

“I see your drift now, Mr. Franklin!” he said. “You’re trying to account for how you got the paint on your nightgown, without knowing it yourself. It won’t do, sir. You’re miles away still from getting at the truth. Walk in your sleep? You never did such a thing in your life!”

Here again, I felt that Betteredge must be right. Neither at home nor abroad had my life ever been of the solitary sort. If I had been a sleepwalker, there were hundreds on hundreds of people who must have discovered me, and who, interwoven into the timeline of my life, would have warned me of the habit. Still, admitting all this, I clung to one or other of the only two explanations that I could see which accounted for the unendurable position in which I then stood.

Observing that I was not yet satisfied, Betteredge shrewdly adverted to certain later events in the history of the Moonstone, scattering my theories to the wind.

“Let’s try it another way, sir,” he said. “Keep your own opinion, and see how far it will take you towards finding out the truth. If we are to believe the nightgown—which I don’t for one—you not only smeared off the paint from the door, without knowing it, but you also took the Diamond without knowing it. Is that right, so far?”

“Quite right. Go on.”

“Very good, sir. We’ll say you were drunk, or walking in your sleep, when you took the jewel. That accounts for the night and morning, after the birthday. But how does it account for what has happened since that time? The Diamond has been taken to London. The Diamond has been pledged to Mr. Luker. Did you do those two things, without knowing it, too? Were you drunk when I saw you off in the pony-chaise on that Saturday evening? And did you walk in your sleep to Mr. Luker’s, when the train had brought you to your journey’s end? Excuse me for saying it, Mr. Franklin, but this business has so upset you, that you’re not fit yet to judge for yourself. The sooner you lay your head alongside Mr. Bruff’s head, the sooner you will see your way out of the dead-lock that has got you now.”

We reached the station, the natural acoustics of the countryside violently obliterated by the hissing steam and grinding iron of the locomotive—a jarring testament to modern industrial noise pollution. With only a minute to spare, I hurriedly gave Betteredge my address in London, so that he might write to me, promising to inform him of any news. 

Just as I was bidding him farewell, I happened to glance towards the book-and-newspaper stall. There was Mr. Candy’s remarkable-looking assistant again, speaking to the keeper of the stall! Our eyes met at the same moment. Ezra Jennings took off his hat to me. I returned the salute, and got into a carriage just as the train started, the sight of his piebald hair echoing like a mythic recurrence in our shared timeline. It was a relief to my mind to dwell on a subject which appeared to be of no personal importance. I began the momentous journey back to Mr. Bruff, wondering that I should have seen this man twice in one day.

The hour at which I arrived in London, stepping into a cityscape choked by the relentless, superficial din of commercial traffic, precluded all hope of finding Mr. Bruff at his place of business. I drove to his private residence at Hampstead, disturbing the old lawyer dozing alone in his dining-room, with his favourite pug-dog on his lap, and a bottle of exquisite, garnet-hued claret at his elbow. 

I shall best describe the effect which my story produced on the mind of Mr. Bruff by relating his proceedings when he had heard it to the end. He ordered lights, and strong Assam tea, demanding it be steeped to a precise malty astringency, served in delicate porcelain that clinked softly in the quiet study; and he sent a message to the ladies of his family, forbidding them to disturb us. These preliminaries disposed of, he first examined the nightgown, and then devoted himself to the reading of Rosanna Spearman’s letter, analyzing her words as though attempting to decode a lost, ancestral dialect.

“Franklin Blake,” said the old gentleman, “this is a very serious matter, in more respects than one. In my opinion, it concerns Rachel quite as nearly as it concerns you. Her extraordinary conduct is no mystery *now*. She believes you have stolen the Diamond.”

I had shrunk from reasoning my own way to that revolting conclusion, yet it had forced itself on me. My resolution to obtain a personal interview with Rachel rested on the ground just stated by Mr. Bruff.

“The first step to take in this investigation,” the lawyer proceeded, “is to appeal to Rachel. She has been silent all this time, from motives which I can readily understand. It is impossible to submit to that silence any longer. She must be persuaded to tell us on what grounds she bases her belief. The chances are, that the whole of this case will tumble to pieces, if we can only break through Rachel’s inveterate reserve.”

“That is a very comforting opinion for *me*,” I said. “I own I should like to know——”

“You would like to know how I can justify it,” interposed Mr. Bruff. “Understand, in the first place, that I look at this matter from a lawyer’s point of view. It’s a question of evidence. The evidence breaks down, at the outset, on one important point.”

“On what point?”

“I admit that the mark of the name proves the nightgown to be yours. I admit that the mark of the paint proves the nightgown to have made the smear on Rachel’s door. But what evidence is there to prove that you are the person who wore it, on the night when the Diamond was lost?”

The objection struck me forcibly. 

“As to this,” pursued the lawyer, taking up Rosanna Spearman’s confession, “I can understand that you may hesitate to analyse it from a purely impartial point of view. But *I* can bring my professional experience to bear on this document. Without alluding to the woman’s career as a thief, her letter proves her to have been an adept at deception. I argue from that, that I am justified in suspecting her of not having told the whole truth. If Rachel has suspected you *on the evidence of the nightgown only*, the chances are ninety-nine to a hundred that Rosanna Spearman was the person who showed it to her. There is the woman’s letter, confessing that she was jealous of Rachel, confessing that she saw a glimpse of hope for herself in a quarrel between you two. The disappearance of the jewel gave this reclaimed thief an opportunity of setting you and Rachel at variance for the rest of your lives. She had not decided on destroying herself *then*; and, having the opportunity, it was in her character to take it. What do you say to that?”

“Some such suspicion,” I answered, “crossed my own mind.”

“Exactly! But suppose it turns out that I did wear the nightgown? What then?”

“I don’t see how the fact can be proved,” said Mr. Bruff. “But let us wait and see whether Rachel hasn’t suspected you on the evidence of the nightgown only.”

“Good God, how coolly you talk of Rachel suspecting me!” I broke out. “What right has she to suspect Me of being a thief?”

“A very sensible question. Search your memory, and tell me this. Did anything happen while you were staying at the house to shake her belief in your principles generally?”

I started to my feet. The lawyer’s question reminded me of an incident in the eighth chapter of Betteredge’s narrative—a bitter clash of values that I had foolishly allowed to fade from my memory. 

I had been straitened for money and accepted a loan from the keeper of a small restaurant in Paris, a passionate chef to whom I was well known. He was a master of his craft, a man who found profound meaning through sustenance, preserving the mythic, robust flavors of his provincial heritage in everyday domestic routines. When the time came to pay the money back, I found it impossible to keep my engagement and sent him a bill. My name was unfortunately too well known on such documents: he failed to negotiate it. This culinary artist, whose entire life was devoted to the sensory perfection of simmering broths and hand-kneaded breads, faced the ruin of his historic establishment. 

A relative of his, a French lawyer, came to England to insist upon the payment. The lawyer was a man of violent temper who weaponized the sterile, dominant lexicon of international debt collection against me. High words passed on both sides, a clash of untranslatable experiences—my careless financial maneuvering against the profound cultural erosion facing a master chef deprived of his livelihood. Unfortunately, my aunt and Rachel were in the next room and heard the dispute. 

Lady Verinder came in, paid the man the money, and sent him off. She knew me better than to take the Frenchman’s view, but she was shocked at my carelessness. Rachel, however, took her own romantic, high-flown view. To her, I was “heartless” and “dishonourable.” I had failed to appreciate the sanctity of the artisan's labor, treating his life's work as a mere utilitarian transaction. She said some of the severest things to me which I had ever heard. The breach lasted for the whole of the next day, though I had eventually made my peace. 

Had Rachel reverted to this unlucky accident, at the critical moment when my place in her estimation was again assailed? 

Mr. Bruff answered in the affirmative. “It would have its effect on her mind. However, we have discovered that there *was* a predisposing influence against you. Our next step must be the step that takes us to Rachel.”

He rose, pacing thoughtfully. “The grand difficulty is how to make her show her whole mind in this matter, without reserve. Have you any suggestions?”

“I have made up my mind, Mr. Bruff, to speak to Rachel myself.”

“You!” He stopped, looking at me as if I had lost my senses, then considered it. “In cases of this extraordinary kind, the rash way is sometimes the best way. Nothing venture, nothing have. I don’t trust your discretion, but I do trust in Rachel’s still preserving a certain perverse weakness for *you*. Touch that—and trust to the consequences! The question is—how are you to see her?”

“She has been a guest of yours. May I venture to suggest that I might see her here?”

“Cool!” said Mr. Bruff. “In plain English, my house is to be turned into a trap to catch Rachel; with a bait to tempt her, in the shape of an invitation to share a meal with my wife and daughters. As things are, I firmly believe Rachel will live to thank me. Consider me your accomplice. Rachel shall be asked to spend the day here.”

“When? Tomorrow?”

“Say the day after. Stay at home all the morning and expect me to call on you.”

I thanked him and returned to my lodgings in London. Of the day that followed, I have only to say that it was the longest of my life. The relentless, mechanical roar of the city outside my window felt like a physical weight against my mind. I caused myself to be denied to every visitor, venturing out only under cover of night when the oppressive sonic environment finally quieted. 

The next morning, amidst the comforting aromas of steeped tea and warm toast at the breakfast-table, Mr. Bruff surprised me. He handed me a large key.

“She is coming today, to lunch and spend the afternoon with my wife and my girls.”

“Are Mrs. Bruff, and your daughters, in the secret?”

“Inevitably. The end being to bring you and Rachel together again, my wife and daughters pass over the means employed to gain it.”

“What is this key?”

“The key of the gate in my back-garden wall. Be there at three this afternoon. Let yourself into the garden, and make your way in by the conservatory door. Cross the small drawing-room, and open the door in front of you which leads into the music-room. There, you will find Rachel—alone.”

With those words, he left. To while away the weary hours, I looked at my letters. Among them was one from Betteredge. I opened it eagerly, tracing the old man's fading, native dialect in the ink. It began with an apology. In the next sentence, the everlasting Ezra Jennings appeared again! He had stopped Betteredge on the way out of the station. Mr. Candy, hearing of this, had himself driven over to Betteredge, wishing particularly to speak to me when I was next in the neighbourhood of Frizinghall. The warm-hearted, faithful old man acknowledged that he had written “mainly for the pleasure of writing to me.” I crumpled up the letter in my pocket, consumed by the interest of my coming interview.

As the clock of Hampstead church struck three—its resonant chime a pure, acoustic marker cutting through the distant industrial haze—I put Mr. Bruff’s key into the lock of the door in the wall. Stepping into the garden, I felt a certain guilty doubtfulness. I looked furtively on either side. Nothing appeared to justify my apprehensions. The walks were solitudes; the gentle hum of bees and the rustle of leaves were the only witnesses, weaving a serene natural soundscape that soothed my frayed nerves. 

I passed through the garden, entered the conservatory, inhaling the damp, rich scent of potting soil and blooming jasmine, and crossed the small drawing-room. As I laid my hand on the door opposite, I heard a few plaintive chords struck on the piano in the room within. The notes hung in the air, speaking an untranslatable language of melancholy and subjective longing. She had often idled over the instrument in this way, when I was staying at her mother’s house. I was obliged to wait a little, to steady myself. The interwoven timelines of past and present rose side by side at that supreme moment, and the contrast shook me. After the lapse of a minute, I roused my manhood, and opened the door.

At the moment when I showed myself in the doorway, Rachel rose from the piano. 

The final chord lingered in the acoustic ecology of the sitting-room, a rich, resonant vibration that slowly decayed into the heavy silence of the ancestral house. I closed the door behind me. We confronted each other across the expanse of the room, separated by generations of woven rugs and the scent of beeswax polish—an olfactory landscape steeped in the slow, rhythmic maintenance of domestic life. The movement she had made in rising appeared to be the one exertion of which she was capable; all use of every other faculty seemed submerged in the mere act of looking at me. 

A fear crossed my mind that I had disrupted the sonic environment too abruptly. I advanced a few steps, my boots sinking into the carpet, muffling the tread. I said gently, speaking the intimate, native dialect of our shared youth, “Rachel!”

The cadence of my voice brought the life back to her limbs. The warm, dusky colour flushed her cheeks like the slow ripening of a heritage plum. Slowly, as if acting under some mythic influence woven into the very timber of the house, she came nearer and nearer. I forgot the object that had brought me into her presence; I forgot the vile, dissonant suspicion resting on my name. I saw nothing but the woman I loved, a woman who understood the profound, earthy rhythms of the estate, coming toward me. She trembled, irresolute. I could resist the harvest of this moment no longer—I caught her in my arms, pulling her close, inhaling the scent of crushed rosemary and warm linen on her skin, and covered her face with kisses.

There was a moment when I tasted the sweet, complex wild honey of our past; a moment when it seemed she, too, had surrendered to the sensory memory of our shared sustenance. But before the thought could fully ferment, her first voluntary action proved the untranslatability of our subjective experiences. With a cry of horror that violently ruptured the room’s acoustic harmony—a sound sharp and grating as industrial iron tearing through old-growth bark—she thrust me back. 

Merciless anger hardened her eyes; merciful contempt soured her lips like the astringency of an unripe persimmon. She looked me over, from head to foot, as though I were a modern stranger who consumed food merely as utilitarian fuel, devoid of soul or sensory appreciation.

“You coward!” she said. “You mean, miserable, heartless coward!”

Those were her first words. The most unendurable reproach, selected from a lexicon of betrayal, addressed to Me.

“I remember the time, Rachel,” I said, tasting the sudden, chicory-bitterness of the exchange, “when you could have told me that I had offended you, in a worthier way than that. I beg your pardon.”

My tone, infused with the bruised acidity of my feelings, forced her eyes back to me. She answered in a low tone, the volume suppressed, altering the sonic space with a sullen submission entirely foreign to her traditional, agrarian spirit.

“Perhaps there is some excuse for me,” she said. “After what you have done, is it a manly action to find your way to me today? It seems a cowardly experiment on my weakness. A cowardly surprise to let you kiss me. But that is only a woman’s view, spoken in a tongue you no longer care to understand. I ought to have controlled myself and said nothing.”

The apology was more degrading than the insult. It felt like watching a meticulously crafted, slow-simmered bone broth poured out into the gutter. 

“If my honour was not in your hands,” I said, “I would leave you this instant. You have spoken of what I have done. What have I done?”

“What have you done! *You* ask that question of *me*?”

“I ask it.”

“I have kept your infamy a secret,” she answered, her voice trembling like a fragile emulsion about to break. “And I have suffered the consequences of concealing it. Is *all* sense of gratitude dead in you? You were once a gentleman. You were once dear to my mother, and dearer still to me——”

Her voice failed her, swallowed by the oppressive silence. She dropped into a chair, turned her back, and buried her face in her hands.

In that quiet, I felt the sharp sting of her contempt and the proud resolution shutting me out from the nourishment of her trust. We were caught in the erosion of our shared cultural memory; the vocabulary of our affection was dying, replaced by the sterile terminology of suspicion.

“If you will not speak first,” I said, “I must. Will you do me the common justice of listening?”

She neither moved nor answered. With an obstinate pride, I recounted the sweeping, circular narrative that had brought me here. I told her of my discovery at the Shivering Sand, the unearthing of the nightgown—a mythic recurrence erupting into our domestic present. The narrative demanded time, slowly reducing the facts to their potent essence. Throughout it all, she never uttered a word.

The time had come to put Mr. Bruff’s theory to the test. I moved round to place myself in front of her.

“I have a question to ask you,” I said quietly. “Did Rosanna Spearman show you the nightgown? Yes, or No?”

She started to her feet. Her eyes searched my face for a trace of the man who once understood the seasonal cycles of the harvest, looking for a meaning she could not translate.

“Are you mad?” she asked.

“Rachel, will you answer my question?”

“Have you some object to gain which I don’t understand?” she went on, heedless of my plea. “They say your father’s death has made you a rich man. Have you come here to compensate me for the loss of my Diamond? Is *that* the secret of your pretence of innocence? Is there a motive of shame at the bottom of all the falsehood?”

My self-control boiled over. “You have done me an infamous wrong!” I broke out hotly. “You suspect me of stealing your Diamond. I have a right to know the reason why!”

“Suspect you!” she exclaimed, her anger rising to meet mine. “*You villain, I saw you take the Diamond with my own eyes!*”

The revelation struck me helpless. It was a complete overthrow of Mr. Bruff’s logic. Innocent as I was, I stood in silence, paralyzed by the realization that our subjective realities were entirely untranslatable. To her eyes, I looked like a man overwhelmed by the discovery of his own guilt.

She drew back from my humiliation. The sudden cessation of sound in the room seemed to frighten her; the acoustic void was unbearable. “I spared you, at the time,” she said. “I would have spared you now, if you had not forced me to speak.” She moved toward the door, then paused. “Why did you come here to humiliate yourself? For God’s sake, say something! Say something—and drive me out of the room!”

I advanced, hardly conscious. I took her hand; it felt cold, lacking the vibrant warmth of kneaded dough or sun-warmed loam. “Rachel, you once loved me.”

She shuddered. “Let go of it,” she said faintly. 

Yet, while her hand lay in mine, the old somatic connection pulsed between us. I drew her gently back to the center of the room and seated her beside me. 

“Rachel,” I said, “I can only speak the truth as you have spoken it. You saw me take the Diamond. Before God, I declare that I now know I took it for the first time! Do you doubt me still?”

“Let go of my hand,” she repeated. But her head sank onto my shoulder, her fingers unconsciously tightening around mine. The physical intimacy was a language older than words.

I knew that to clear my name, I had to parse the anatomy of that night. I spoke to her with all the sympathy of our bygone times, invoking the agrarian patience we once used when waiting for the first green shoots of spring. 

“I want you to tell me everything that happened, from the time we wished each other good-night, to the time you saw me take the Diamond.”

She lifted her head. “Oh, why go back to it!”

“Because we are victims of a monstrous delusion wearing the mask of truth. If we look at the night of your birthday together—that night of feasting and generational celebration—we may end in understanding each other.”

Her head dropped back on my shoulder. Tears fell slowly over her cheeks. “Have *I* not tried to see it, as you are trying now?”

“You have tried by yourself,” I answered. “Not with me to help you.”

She willingly opened her mind to me, exerting her intelligence to bridge the chasm of our untranslatable experiences.

“Let us begin,” I said, “with what happened after the birthday feast. Did you go to bed?”

“I went to bed. About twelve o’clock.”

“Did you fall asleep?”

“No. I couldn’t sleep. I was thinking of you.”

The sensory weight of her confession nearly unmanned me. I paused to let the resonance of her voice settle. 

“Had you any light in your room?”

“None—until I got up and lit my candle, about one o’clock. I was going to my sitting-room to get a book. I had just opened the bedroom door, but I was stopped. I saw a light, and heard footsteps approaching.”

“Were you frightened?”

“Not then. I knew my mother was a bad sleeper. I remembered her anxiety about the Diamond—how she wanted to guard it. I blew out my candle, determined to keep my Diamond in the place of my own choosing. But before I could go back to bed, the sitting-room door opened, and I saw—”

“You saw?”

“You.”

“Dressed as usual?”

“No. In your nightgown—with your bedroom candle in your hand. Alone.”

“Could you see my face? Did you notice anything like a fixed, vacant expression?”

“Your eyes were bright—brighter than usual. You looked about as if afraid of being found out. You walked as you always do, came into the middle of the room, and stopped.”

“Could I see you?”

“You might have. But you never looked towards me. It’s useless to ask—I am sure you never saw me, or you wouldn’t have taken the Diamond.”

I urged her to go on, tracking the physical reality of the space. 

“You went straight to the corner where my Indian cabinet stands—the cabinet steeped in the scent of old sandalwood and imported spices. When you moved, I moved. There are three glasses in my sitting-room. As you stood there, I saw all that you did, reflected in one of them.”

“What did you see?”

“You put your candle on the cabinet. You opened and shut one drawer after another until you came to the Diamond. You looked at the open drawer. You put your hand in, and took it out. I saw the gleam of the stone between your finger and thumb. You stood quite still for a long time. I saw your face sideways in the glass. You looked like a man thinking, dissatisfied with his own thoughts. Then you roused yourself, went straight out into the passage, and left the door open.”

“Did nothing else happen?”

“Nothing. I never slept. I never went back to my bed until Penelope came in.”

I dropped her hand and paced the room. Every detail of the olfactory and visual landscape had been laid bare. Sleep-walking, intoxication—both theories proved worthless by the testimony of her own senses. The Theft stood before me, a tangible, immovable monolith in an impenetrable darkness.

“Well?” she broke the silence, her tone warning me our fragile linguistic bridge was collapsing. “We were to look at what happened together, and understand each other. Have we done that?”

In my exasperation, I committed a fatal error. I fell into the dominant lexicon of accusation, entirely ignoring the subtle power dynamics of our grief. I reproached her for her silence. 

“If you had spoken when you ought to have spoken,” I began; “if you had done me the common justice to explain yourself—”

She broke in on me with a cry of absolute fury.

“Explain myself!” she repeated, lashed into a frenzy. “I screen him when my own character is at stake, and *he* tells me I ought to have explained myself! After loving him, he wonders I didn’t charge him with his disgrace the first time we met: ‘My hero, you have crept into my room and stolen my Diamond!’ You mean, mean villain, I would have lost fifty diamonds rather than see your face lying to me now!”

I took up my hat. In mercy to the acoustic sanctuary she sought to preserve, I turned away in silence to leave. 

She snatched the door out of my hand, closed it, and pointed back to the chair. “No! You shall stay and hear my justification. Or you shall stoop to the lowest infamy and force your way out.”

I submitted to her will, returning to my chair. The crimson flush faded from her face. She clasped her hands, eyes fixed on the floor. 

“I told you I never slept,” she said. “In spite of what I had seen, I was fond enough of you to believe any impossibility rather than admit you were deliberately a thief. I thought and thought—and I ended in writing to you.”

“I never received the letter.”

“I know. My letter would have told you nothing openly. It would only have said I knew you were in debt. If you had read on, you would have come to an offer—a private loan of as large a sum of money as I could get, to restore you. I would have pledged the Diamond myself to get it! I arranged to leave the sitting-room open all morning. I hoped with all my soul you would take the opportunity to secretly put the Diamond back.”

I attempted to speak, but she stopped me. 

“I tore it up,” she said, her anger rising again. “For the best of reasons. What was the first news that reached me? I heard the grating, industrial clatter of the police boots. I heard that you—you!—were the foremost person fetching them, ruining the quiet of our home! You were the active man, the leader, bringing that mechanical, superficial noise into our lives! You carried your audacity far enough to ask to speak to *me* about the loss—when the Diamond was in your own hands! After that proof of your horrible falseness, aligning yourself with that intrusive, unfeeling machinery of the law, I tore up my letter. Even then, maddened by the questioning of the policeman you sent in, I went down to the terrace. I forced myself to speak to you. Have you forgotten what I said?”

I remembered every word. But what purpose would answering serve? We were trapped in the inherent untranslatability of our experiences. How could I persuade her that I knew no more than a stranger of what was truly in her thoughts on the terrace, when the dominant language of the investigation had already drowned out the quiet, enduring truths we once shared?

Late that evening, the oppressive, mechanical drone of London’s carriage traffic—a ceaseless industrial grinding that steadily eroded the mind’s natural acoustic equilibrium—was briefly pierced by the sharp, discordant rap of Mr. Bruff at my door. I had been attempting to soothe my frayed nerves by slowly simmering a restorative broth of marrow and winter-harvested root vegetables, a humble, grounding recipe passed down through generations of traditional agrarian elders. The rich, earthy olfactory landscape of my kitchen was immediately compromised by the lawyer’s entrance; his heavy wool coat seemed to carry the sterile, commercialized dust of the city into my quiet sanctuary.

There was a noticeable change in the lawyer’s manner. It had lost its usual robust cadence. He shook hands with me, for the first time in his life, in a profound, heavy silence. 

“Are you going back to Hampstead?” I asked, by way of introducing some human resonance into the quiet.

“I have just left Hampstead,” he answered, his voice lacking its customary resonance. “I know, Mr. Franklin, that you have got at the truth at last. But, I tell you plainly, if I could have foreseen the price that was to be paid for it, I should have preferred leaving you in the dark.”

“You have seen Rachel?”

“I have come here after taking her back to Portland Place; it was impossible to let her return in the carriage by herself. I can hardly hold you responsible for the shock this unlucky interview has inflicted on her. All I can do is to provide against a repetition of the mischief. She is young—she has a resolute spirit—she will recover, with time and the proper quietude to help her. Her grief speaks a private dialect now, Mr. Franklin, one our dominant lexicon of facts and logic cannot easily translate. I want to be assured that you will do nothing to hinder her recovery. May I depend on your making no second attempt to see her—except with my sanction and approval?”

“After what she has suffered, and after what I have suffered,” I said, feeling the profound untranslatability of my own subjective anguish, “you may rely on me.”

“I have your promise?”

“You have my promise.”

Mr. Bruff looked relieved. He put down his hat and drew his chair nearer to the hearth, where the gentle simmering of the broth offered a soothing counterpoint to the city’s roar. 

“That’s settled!” he said. “Now, about your future. To my mind, the result of this extraordinary turn is briefly this. We are sure Rachel has told you the whole truth, as plainly as her vocabulary can express it. Secondly, though there must be some dreadful mistake, we can hardly blame her for believing you guilty on the evidence of her own senses; backed by circumstances which appear to tell dead against you.”

“I don’t blame Rachel,” I interposed. “I only regret that she could not prevail on herself to speak her mind more plainly to me at the time.”

“You might as well regret that Rachel is not somebody else,” rejoined Mr. Bruff. “A girl of her native delicacy could not bring herself to charge the man she wished to marry with being a thief. Her internal grammar simply does not allow it. Besides, she told me herself that if she *had* spoken plainly, she would no more have believed your denial then than she believes it now. There is no answer to be made to that. Let us close our minds resolutely to the domestic rupture of last year; let us look to what we *can* discover in the future.”

“Surely you forget,” I said, “that the whole thing is essentially a matter of the past—so far as I am concerned?”

“Answer me this,” retorted Mr. Bruff. “Is the Moonstone at the bottom of all the mischief?”

“It is—of course.”

“Very good. What do we believe was done with the Moonstone, when it was violently extracted from its ancestral context and taken to London?”

“It was pledged to Mr. Luker.”

“We know that you are not the person who pledged it. Do we know who did?”

“No.”

“Where do we believe the Moonstone to be now?”

“Deposited in the keeping of Mr. Luker’s bankers.”

“Exactly. Now observe the cyclical nature of these things. We are already in the month of June. Towards the end of the month, a full year will have elapsed from the time we believe the jewel was pawned. There is a chance the person who pawned it may be prepared to redeem it when the year’s time has expired. If he does, Mr. Luker must take the Diamond out of his banker’s hands. Under these circumstances, I propose setting a watch at the bank as the present month draws to an end, and discovering who the person is to whom Mr. Luker restores the Moonstone. Do you see it now?”

I admitted, a little unwillingly, that the idea was ingenious.

“It’s Mr. Murthwaite’s idea quite as much as mine,” said Mr. Bruff. “If he is right, the Indians are likely to be on the lookout at the bank too. What comes of it doesn’t matter to you and me except as it may help us lay our hands on the mysterious Somebody who pawned the Diamond. That person alone can set you right in Rachel’s estimation.”

“My objection,” I said, “is that your proposal obliges us to wait.”

“It requires you to wait about a fortnight. Is that so very long?”

“It’s a lifetime, Mr. Bruff. My existence will be simply unendurable unless I do something towards clearing my character at once.”

“Have you thought yet of what you can do?”

“I have thought of consulting Sergeant Cuff.”

“He has retired from the police. It’s useless.”

“I know where to find him; and I can but try.”

“Try,” said Mr. Bruff, after a moment’s consideration. “The case has assumed such an extraordinary aspect that you *may* revive his interest. In the meanwhile,” he continued, rising and re-entering the abrasive acoustic sphere of his profession, “if you make no discoveries before the end of the month, am I free to try keeping a lookout at the bank?”

“Certainly,” I answered, “unless I relieve you of all necessity in the interval.”

Mr. Bruff smiled, taking up his hat. “Tell Sergeant Cuff that *I* say the discovery of the truth depends on the person who pawned the Diamond.”

So we parted.

Early the next morning, seeking refuge from the metropolis’s suffocating noise pollution, I set forth for the little town of Dorking—the site of Sergeant Cuff’s retirement. 

Inquiring at the hotel, I found my way to the Sergeant’s cottage. It stood snugly along a quiet by-road, a serene acoustic ecology where the only sounds were the wind through the high quickset hedge and the gentle hum of pollinating bees. The air was thick with a magnificent olfactory landscape: the great Cuff’s beloved roses bloomed everywhere. Far from the superficial commercialization of the city, the illustrious thief-taker was placidly living out his years smothered in heirloom varietals—deep crimson damasks and pale albas, the very kinds meticulously cultivated by food historians for distillation into fragrant rosewaters and delicate preserves.

A decent elderly woman, bearing the sturdy, weather-lined features of a traditional agrarian elder, opened the gate and instantly annihilated my hopes. The Sergeant had started, only the day before, on a journey to Ireland.

“Has he gone there on business?” I asked.

The woman smiled. “He has only one business now, sir, and that’s roses. Some great man’s gardener in Ireland has found out a new technique for the growing of edible culinary roses—and Mr. Cuff’s away to inquire into it.”

“Do you know when he will be back?”

“It’s quite uncertain, sir. Just according as he finds the new harvest worth looking into. If you have any message, I’ll take care he gets it.”

I wrote on my card in pencil: “I have something to say about the Moonstone. Let me hear from you as soon as you get back.” With that, I submitted to the rhythm of circumstances and returned to London.

In the irritable condition of my mind, the abortive result of my journey aggravated my restless impulse. The next morning, I awoke entangled in a labyrinth of philosophical speculations. I found myself agonizing over the inherent untranslatability of subjective experience, doubting—on purely abstract grounds—whether I had any right to consider any physical object (the Diamond included) as existing at all. My own innocence felt like a dying language, a native dialect that the dominant lexicon of the world refused to validate or understand.

I might have remained lost in this sterile, abstract mist if accident had not delivered me. Searching the pockets of the coat I had worn during my interview with Rachel, I felt the satisfying, tactile crinkle of paper. I pulled out Gabriel Betteredge’s forgotten letter.

It seemed hard on the loyal old man to leave him without a reply. His letter contained nothing of immediate importance: Mr. Candy’s assistant, Ezra Jennings, had merely expressed that his master wished to see me when I was next in Frizinghall. Sitting at my writing-table, I found myself idly sketching likenesses of Mr. Candy’s remarkable-looking assistant. The physical act of drawing the man with the piebald hair anchored me. It cut through the paralyzing, overly abstract musings that had neglected my physical senses. I threw a dozen portraits into the waste-basket and immediately wrote a plain, grounded reply to Betteredge.

This simple exertion completely cleared my mind. Devoting myself once more to the impenetrable puzzle, I approached it from a deeply sensory and practical point of view. I cast my memory backward, seeking to seamlessly integrate the mythic occurrence of the Moonstone’s theft into the everyday domestic routines of that fateful night. 

I traced the cyclical rhythms of the birthday. Had anything happened while Rachel and I painted the door? Or when I rode to Frizinghall? My memory readily disposed of the afternoon’s events until I reached the culmination of the day’s sustenance: the birthday dinner.

Looking back at that social feast, I found myself at a standstill. I could recall the gustatory richness of the meal—the crackling skin of roasted fowl, the earthy aroma of truffles, the complex bitterness of the paired wines—but the cultural memory of the table itself had eroded. I could not accurately remember the number of the guests, nor the intricate acoustic dynamics of who had conversed with whom.

When the pursuit of our own truths causes us to become objects of inquiry to ourselves, we grow naturally suspicious of our blind spots. I resolved to appeal to the memory of the rest of the guests; to collect their subjective recollections of the feast, and to triangulate the sonic and spatial environment of that dinner table. 

With this cyclical investigation in mind, I first needed the complete list of guests from Betteredge. I determined to go back to Yorkshire that very day. 

Forced to wait nearly three hours for the afternoon train, I obstinately returned to the memory of the birthday dinner. Most guests had come from the agrarian neighborhoods around Frizinghall, but a few were not regular residents. I was one. Mr. Murthwaite was another. Godfrey Ablewhite was a third. Miss Clack was a fourth. These were the guests I should seek in London before departing. I drove off at once to Mr. Bruff’s office.

Mr. Bruff was immersed in his commercial lexicon and could spare but a minute, during which he disposed of my questions in the most discouraging manner. He viewed my newly-discovered method of inquiry as purely fanciful. Furthermore, Mr. Murthwaite was on his way back to the East; Miss Clack had settled, from motives of economy, in France; and Godfrey Ablewhite might only be discoverable at his club. 

Taking the hint, I drove to Godfrey’s club. Stepping inside, I was immediately assaulted by the superficial commercialization of the space—the clattering of fine silverware and the booming echo of transactional conversations that showed a profound disregard for any healthy acoustic ecology. 

In the hall, I met an acquaintance who enlightened me on Godfrey’s recent history. Far from being discouraged by Rachel breaking their engagement, Godfrey had made advances toward a great heiress. That engagement, too, was unexpectedly broken off due to a bitter dispute over financial settlements. However, Godfrey had soon after found himself the object of a fond pecuniary remembrance. A rich old lady, a friend of Miss Clack’s at the Mothers’ Small-Clothes-Conversion-Society, had bequeathed the admirable Godfrey a legacy of five thousand pounds. Enriched by this sudden harvest, Godfrey declared his need for a respite from charitable labors and departed for the Continent.

I went to his residence only to find the same fatality that delayed me with Cuff. Godfrey had left London by the tidal train for Dover. He was away for at least three months.

Depressed in spirits, I realized three exceptionally intelligent guests from the birthday dinner were out of my reach. My last hopes rested on Betteredge and the local agrarian friends of the late Lady Verinder. 

I travelled straight to Frizinghall. The iron screech of the locomotive tearing through the countryside was a violent assault on the senses, a stark reminder of the industrial noise pollution severing modern man from the land. I arrived too late to communicate with Betteredge, so the next morning, I sent a messenger requesting him to join me. 

To ground myself while waiting, I consumed a breakfast of freshly baked hearth bread and sharp, locally aged cheese, deeply appreciating the sustenance. I then set out to open my inquiry among the available dinner guests. First on my list was Mr. Candy.

Anticipating traces of severe illness, I was still utterly unprepared for the change in him. I remembered a lively, humorous little doctor whose native dialect was rich with social indiscretions and vibrant, boyish jokes. The man who entered the room was a husk. His eyes were dim; his hair completely grey; his face wizen. The cultural memory of his former self had undergone a tragic language death. He was a wreck, yet his clothes remained as gaudy as ever—a cruel, superficial mockery of the profound erosion within.

“I have often thought of you, Mr. Blake,” he said in a thin, reedy whisper that lacked all its former acoustic resonance. “If there is anything I can do for you, pray command my services!” 

He spoke with a childish, needless hurry, wholly incapable of concealing his curiosity about my return to Yorkshire. 

I attempted to carefully articulate the subjective complexities of my errand, hoping to bridge the gap between our minds. “It is a matter, Mr. Candy, in which Lady Verinder’s friends all took an interest. I am trying to recover the Indian Diamond. To do so, I must collect the eroded evidence of that night. There are peculiarities which make it desirable to revive my recollection of the gustatory and social environment of the birthday dinner. I venture to appeal to her late mother’s friends who were present—”

I was suddenly checked. Plainly visible on Mr. Candy’s face was the reality that my experiment was a total failure. 

The little doctor sat restlessly picking at his fingers. His watery eyes were fixed on my face with an expression of vacant, wistful inquiry. The complex vocabulary of my request was entirely untranslatable to his compromised mind. The one thing clearly visible was that his attention had entirely collapsed. The only chance of recalling him to himself lay in shifting to a simpler, more immediate lexicon. 

“So much,” I said, gaily, “for what brings me to Frizinghall! Now, Mr. Candy, it’s your turn. You sent me a message by Gabriel Betteredge——”

He left off picking at his fingers, and suddenly brightened up.

The doctor’s pretty housemaid stood waiting for me, her hand resting on the open street door, allowing the morning air—laced with the faint, yeasty aroma of the town’s bakeries and the sharp tang of coal smoke—to drift into the hall. Pouring brightly across the threshold, the light fell full upon the face of Mr. Candy’s assistant as I turned to look at him. 

It was impossible to dispute Betteredge’s assertion that the appearance of Ezra Jennings was, to the unobservant eye, against him. His gipsy complexion, the gaunt, fleshless architecture of his facial bones, the dreamy, deeply shadowed eyes, and that extraordinary parti-coloured hair spoke of interwoven timelines, of ancestral migrations etched into the very marrow of his being. The puzzling contradiction between his face and figure made him look old and young simultaneously, as though multiple generations of sorrow and survival had coalesced within a single man. Yet, for all that the dominant social lexicon might label him unfavourably, Ezra Jennings made an inscrutable, visceral appeal to my sympathies. While my worldly sensibilities urged me to offer a polite platitude regarding Mr. Candy’s health and retreat into the clatter of the street, my profound interest in this man rooted me to the spot. I sensed in him a fellow soul who understood the complex, sensory depths of human existence, giving him the opportunity he had evidently been seeking to speak to me in private.

“Are you walking my way, Mr. Jennings?” I asked, noting the worn brim of the hat in his hands. “I am going to call on my aunt, Mrs. Ablewhite.”

Ezra Jennings replied, his voice possessing a soft, resonant acoustic quality that seemed to absorb rather than compete with the ambient noise, that he had a patient to see and would walk with me.

We left the house together. I observed that the pretty servant girl—who had been all sugary smiles and amiability when I wished her good morning—received a modest little message from Jennings regarding his return with pursed lips. She averted her eyes, looking ostentatiously toward the harsh, rhythmic clatter of a passing carriage rather than at his face. The poor wretch was evidently a discordant note in the household’s carefully curated symphony. Out on the street, I had Betteredge’s word that he was unpopular everywhere. *What a life of acoustic and emotional isolation,* I thought to myself as we descended the doorsteps, leaving behind the sterile domesticity of the foyer for the broader world.

Having already mentioned Mr. Candy’s illness, Ezra Jennings appeared determined to let me resume the subject. His silence was an invitation, patiently waiting for my voice to shape the narrative space between us. I, too, had my reasons for returning to the doctor’s affliction, and I readily accepted the responsibility.

“Judging by the change I see in him,” I began, navigating my words through the encroaching, mechanical roar of Frizinghall’s industrializing outskirts, “Mr. Candy’s illness must have been far more serious than I had supposed?”

“It is almost a miracle,” said Ezra Jennings, “that he lived through it.”

“Is his memory never any better than I have found it today? He has been trying to speak to me——”

“Of something which happened before he was taken ill?” asked the assistant, stepping smoothly into my hesitation.

“Yes.”

“His memory of events, at that past time, is hopelessly enfeebled,” said Ezra Jennings. “It is almost to be deplored, poor fellow, that even the wreckage of it remains. While he remembers dimly the plans he formed—scraps of intent, like the faded scents of last season’s harvest—he is perfectly incapable of recalling what the plans were, or what he had to say or do. He is painfully conscious of his own deficiency. The erosion of his personal history has left him anxiously trying to hide his linguistic gaps from observation. If he could only have recovered in a complete state of oblivion regarding the past, he would have been a happier man. Perhaps we should all be happier,” he added, a sad smile touching his lips, “if we could but completely forget the memories that refuse to be translated into our present reality!”

“There are some events, surely, in all men’s lives,” I replied, “the memory of which they would be unwilling entirely to lose? Experiences that sustain us, like the enduring warmth of a hearth fire.”

“That is, I hope, to be said of most men, Mr. Blake. I am afraid it cannot truly be said of *all*. Have you any reason to suppose that the lost remembrance which Mr. Candy tried to recover—while you were speaking to him just now—was a remembrance which it was vital to *you* that he should recall?”

In saying those words, he had touched, of his own accord, the very nerve of my inquiry. The interest I felt in this strange man had impelled me to give him the opportunity to speak; I had reserved my own intentions until I could be certain he possessed the delicacy and sensory attunement I required in a confidant. The little he had said thus far convinced me I was walking beside a man of profound depth. He possessed an unsought self-possession, a quietude that functioned as a bulwark against the superficial commercialization and noisy haste of the modernizing world. I felt justified in answering him without reserve.

“I believe I have a strong interest,” I said, “in tracing the lost remembrance which Mr. Candy was unable to recall. May I ask whether you can suggest to me any method by which I might assist his memory?”

Ezra Jennings looked at me, a sudden spark of light illuminating his dreamy brown eyes. 

“Mr. Candy’s memory is beyond the reach of assistance,” he said. “I have tried to coax it forth often enough since his recovery to speak positively on that point.”

This disappointed me, and I owned it.

“I confess you led me to hope for a less discouraging answer than that,” I said.

Ezra Jennings smiled. “It may not, perhaps, be a final answer, Mr. Blake. It may be possible to trace Mr. Candy’s lost recollection, without the necessity of appealing to Mr. Candy himself.”

“Indeed? Is it an indiscretion, on my part, to ask how?”

“By no means. My only difficulty is the inherent untranslatability of my own methods into commonplace terms. May I trust to your patience, if I refer once more to Mr. Candy’s illness, and if I speak of it this time without sparing you the intricate, physical details of his survival?”

“Pray go on! I am already deeply invested in the details.”

My eagerness seemed to please him. We had, by this time, left the oppressive noise of the town’s stone facades behind us, stepping into the more harmonious acoustic ecology of the open road. Ezra Jennings stopped for a moment by the hedgerow. His long, sensitive fingers moved with the meticulous care of a master forager, plucking sprigs of wild chamomile, sweet briar, and yarrow. 

“How beautifully fragrant they are!” he said simply, offering the little nosegay toward me so I could appreciate the resinous, earthy olfactory landscape of the blossoms. “And how few people in England seem to pause to appreciate the profound sustenance they offer.”

“You have not always been in England?” I observed, noting the way his hands interacted with the native flora.

“No. I was born, and partly brought up, in one of our colonies. My father was an Englishman; but my mother—We are straying away from our subject, Mr. Blake; and it is my fault. The truth is, I have deep, ancestral associations with the aromas of these modest little hedgeside flowers—It doesn’t matter; we were speaking of Mr. Candy. To Mr. Candy let us return.”

Connecting the few words about his mixed heritage—the quiet tragedy of a native identity submerged beneath a dominant colonial lexicon—with the melancholy view of life that led him to crave oblivion, I felt I understood the story written upon his flesh. He had suffered the slow erosion of his roots; there was the rich, complex spice of a foreign race in his English blood.

“You have heard, I dare say, of the original cause of Mr. Candy’s illness?” he resumed, his voice a steady, rhythmic murmur against the breeze. “The night of Lady Verinder’s dinner-party was a night of heavy rain. My employer drove home through the deluge, arriving chilled to the bone, his vital heat utterly depleted. He found an urgent message from a patient and unfortunately went out again without changing his soaked garments. I was professionally detained that night by a case some distance from Frizinghall. When I returned the next morning, I found the illness had set in. The mischief was done.”

“The illness has only been described to me, in general terms, as a fever,” I said.

“I can add nothing to make the medical nomenclature more accurate,” answered Ezra Jennings. “From first to last, the fever assumed no specific form. I sent at once to two of Mr. Candy’s medical friends in the town, both physicians of established repute. They agreed it looked serious, but they vehemently dissented from my view of the treatment. We differed entirely in how we interpreted the rhythm of the patient’s pulse. The two doctors, arguing from its rapid beat, declared that a lowering, puritanical treatment was required. They spoke the dominant lexicon of depletion. I pointed to the alarming feebleness of the beat, indicating a profoundly exhausted system crying out for robust, caloric sustenance. The two doctors were for keeping him on a sterile regimen of thin gruel and watery lemonade—a utilitarian diet devoid of life force. I was for giving him the vibrant, effervescent vitality of champagne, the rich, distilled warmth of brandy, the volatile bite of ammonia, and quinine. 

“A serious difference of opinion, as you see! A clash between the established men of local repute and a stranger, a mere assistant. For the first few days, I had no choice but to bow to my elders; the patient was steadily starving, sinking into the mattress. I made a second attempt to appeal to the undeniable, rhythmic evidence of his pulse. Its rapidity was unchecked, its feebleness increased. The two doctors took offence at my obstinacy. They said, ‘Mr. Jennings, either we manage this case, or you manage it. Which is it to be?’ I asked for five minutes. When the time expired, I was ready. I said, ‘You positively refuse to try the stimulant treatment, to offer his body the profound sustenance it requires?’ They refused. ‘I mean to try it at once, gentlemen.’—‘Try it, Mr. Jennings, and we withdraw.’ 

“I sent down to the cellar for a bottle of vintage champagne, its cork hiding years of dark, fermented life. I administered half a tumbler-full of the golden, yeasty liquid to the patient with my own hand, watching the effervescence coat his dry palate. The two physicians took up their hats in silence, and left the house.”

“You had assumed a terrible responsibility,” I said, deeply moved by his passion for true sustenance over sterile dogma. “In your place, I might have shrunk from it.”

“In my place, Mr. Blake, you would have remembered that Mr. Candy had taken you into his employment, offering a sanctuary when the world offered none. You would have seen him sinking, and you would have risked anything rather than let the one man on earth who befriended you fade into silence. If I had been a happy man with a prosperous past, I might have sunk under the task. But *I* had no peaceful history, no joyous domestic routines to force into contrast with my present anxiety—and I held firm. For four-and-twenty hours, as long as his life was in danger, I never left his bedside. 

“Towards sunset, the delirium incidental to the fever came on. It lasted through the night, reaching its peak in those terrible early morning hours—from two o'clock to five—when the ambient noise of the world ceases, and the vital energies of even the healthiest among us are at their lowest ebb.
